⏺ The grain isn't the source: its energy is unchanged through the prior and
  final image, and it's already in the data fit only in moving areas, where
  partial frame rejection makes neighbouring Bayer sites get different
  brightness. I'm applying the Bayer-period notch to brightness there, gated by
  the frames-contributing map so static areas stay untouched.

⏺ Both problems had a clear root cause, and both are fixed.
  IMG_20260919_184641_979_LKSR.dng is re-rendered with the fixes (49 s on the
  Mac). Not run on the phone yet, and nothing is committed.

  1. Corners and edges (rail end on the right, trees on the left): wrong motion 
  at the frame edges.
  - How I confirmed it: the smeared corner, solved as its own crop, came out
    sharp, sharper than MFSR. So the solver was fine; the motion it started from
    was wrong.
  - Why: every tile started from one global motion fitted on the whole frame.
    Towards the corners, rotation, lens distortion and rolling shutter make the
    true motion differ from it by more than the solver can correct by itself.
  - Fix: each tile now gets its own coarse Lucas-Kanade alignment, started from
    the global motion. It's only kept if it actually lowers the alignment error,
    so flat tiles like sky keep the global motion.
  - Result: the rail end, the dark object on it and the dry grass are sharp; the
    left-edge trees went from soft and gridded to sharper than MFSR. No
    measurable extra time.

  2. Dot grid in the grass and on the car: not from the grain and not from the 
  start image.
  - How I found it: I measured the Bayer-pattern energy at each solver stage
    (data fit, prior output, final). It's the same at every stage, so neither
    the grain nor the prior adds it, and it's already in the data fit. It only
    shows in moving areas: the static road equals a single-frame demosaic
    (0.0038), the grass is 30% higher and the car 90% higher.
  - Why: where some frames are rejected, each Bayer site ends up measured by a
    different subset of frames, which leaves a regular brightness pattern
    between neighbouring pixels.
  - Fix: the Bayer-period filter, which so far only acted on colour, now also
    cleans brightness, only where frames were rejected. Its strength follows the
    frames-contributing map, so it's zero where all frames contribute.
  - Result: car pattern −30% (down to the static level), the grass dot grid gone
    at 4× zoom, the static road unchanged.
    - Cost: the plate is a touch softer, since that area comes from the
      reference frame alone.
    - The grass only drops 6% overall, because only parts of it are rejected.

  Checks:
  - Static detail: edges +0.5%, +0.4% and +4.5% (night), flat noise −1 to −2%,
    so no regression.
  - Paper-exact parity against Python passes, and the Android build compiles
    (2.1.74).
  - The paper-parity toggle turns both fixes off, along with the other
    improvements.

  Comparison images are in
  /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-monikamalinowska-AndroidStudioProjects-RawLens/
  cec00119-2646-4021-a32e-bf02153239c6/scratchpad/move/:
  - tilelk.png: corner and car, before / now / MFSR;
  - leftedge.png: left-edge trees, before / now / MFSR;
  - lnotch.png: plate and grass, before / after the brightness filter.

⏺ The merge is fine; the blur I saw comes from the noise equalisation pushing
  the prior to denoise harder in moving-water areas, which costs texture on the
  underwater pebbles in daylight. I'm changing that equalisation to scale with
  the burst's noise level, while keeping the brightness dot-pattern filter
  wherever rejection happens.

  Still not right:
  - Moving shallow water in 133434_814 (bottom-left): it comes out softer than
    the single frame; texture is about 0.0032 against 0.0048 in the reference.
    The light patterns through the water differ in every frame, so the merge
    averages them out instead of rejecting them. I tested easing the smoothing
    in rejected areas for daylight; it changed nothing measurable (0.00324 →
    0.00328), so I reverted it. Fixing this properly needs the temporal
    weighting already on the open list for the sea burst. The open sea in other
    bursts is fine.
  - Shaded tree trunk in 111404_842 (left edge): slightly flatter than the
    single frame, but not smeared. That's the clean-daylight look (prior 0.15)
    on a very dark area, a minor point.

⏺ The temporal weights are uniformly soft (about 0.96), so the 7×7 min-filter
  systematically down-weights every frame and slightly softens static detail.
  I'm adding a dead zone so weights stay exactly 1 until disagreement clearly
  exceeds noise.

      19 +// Separable running-sum box blur (radius r, clamped edges), in
         + place.                                                        
      20 +void BoxBlur(float* a, int H, int W, int r, std::vector<float>&
         + tmp) {                                                        
      21 +  tmp.resize(size_t(std::max(H, W)));                          
      22 +  for (int i = 0; i < H; ++i) {                                
      23 +    float* row = a + size_t(i) * W;                            
      24 +    double s = 0;                                              
      25 +    for (int j = -r; j <= r; ++j) s += row[std::min(std::max(j,
         + 0), W - 1)];                                                  
      26 +    for (int j = 0; j < W; ++j) {                              
      27 +      tmp[j] = float(s / (2 * r + 1));                         
      28 +      s += row[std::min(j + r + 1, W - 1)] - row[std::max(j - r
         +, 0)];                                                         
      29 +    }                                                          
      30 +    std::copy(tmp.begin(), tmp.begin() + W, row);              
      31 +  }

      33 +    double s = 0;                                              
      34 +    for (int i = -r; i <= r; ++i) s += a[size_t(std::min(std::m
         +ax(i, 0), H - 1)) * W + j];

      40 +  }

⏺ Night shadow colour now matches the raw truth exactly. One visual check of the
  night burst:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The moving water is fixed and the night red cast is gone, but LK-SR isn't
  perfect yet: one issue is still visible, in the darkest night areas. Paper
  parity still passes and the Android build compiles (2.1.75). Nothing is
  committed. I haven't run anything on the phone.

  Backup: ~/Downloads/RawLens_backup_20261104_1520/ holds a patch of the tracked
  changes, the untracked files and the HEAD hash, taken before any of this.

  What changed:
  - Temporal weighting (the water): each frame is now also compared against the
    reference frame alone, not just the running merge.
    - The test compares local averages over 5×5 pixels. That ignores the
      single-frame demosaic error, which flips sign pixel to pixel, but catches
      real motion.
    - There's a dead zone: a frame keeps full weight until it differs by more
      than 3× the noise. Without it, every frame was slightly down-weighted
      everywhere and static detail softened a little.
    - Result: water texture went from 0.67 to 0.87 of the single frame. Static
      edges stayed within ±0.6% and night within ±0.1%.
  - Exposure compensation: I found brightness steps of up to 4.2% between frames
    in three bursts, from auto-exposure. Each frame is now gain-matched to the
    reference before merging.
  - Night shadow colour: LK-SR had a red cast in night shadows (R/G 1.10 against
    0.98 measured from the raw samples). The FFDNet denoiser was shifting the
    local average brightness and colour. After each denoiser call, large-scale
    colour and brightness are now restored from the data. Shadows now match the
    raw exactly: R/G 0.981, B/G 0.966.

  Three-way check, all 6 bursts at full resolution: single frame vs MFSR
  KernelNet (with our own noise fit) vs LK-SR, at 9 positions each.
  - Detail: LK-SR keeps more than MFSR at almost every position. Texture is
    0.92–1.0 of the single frame on detail areas, against MFSR's 0.85–0.92.
  - Noise: MFSR removes more in daylight (about 0.2–0.5 of the single frame,
    against LK-SR's 0.5–0.8). That's the "DSLR look" setting keeping fine grain,
    not a failure.
  - Night: both remove 80–85% of the noise. LK-SR is sharper on the car, signs
    and lights.
  - No ghosting, chessboard, zipper or smeared corners in any of the 54
    positions I looked at.

  Still open:
  - Darkest night areas (road, bottom of the night burst): LK-SR shows faint
    large-scale colour mottling, soft green and magenta patches, where MFSR
    shows fine neutral noise. This is the next thing to fix.
  - Speed: LK-SR takes 57–84 s per burst on the Mac, up from about 50 s, mostly
    from the extra temporal pass. I haven't re-measured on the phone.

  The new LK-SR and MFSR DNGs for all 6 bursts are in ~/Downloads/LKSR_all/. The
  comparison grids are <burst>_3way.png in
  /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-monikamalinowska-AndroidStudioProjects-RawLens/
  cec00119-2646-4021-a32e-bf02153239c6/scratchpad/all/; each position shows
  single frame | MFSR | LK-SR, left to right.

⏺ The weight passes take most of the GPU time (25.5 s), so I'll compute the
  robust weights once per iteration and reuse them across the z-steps, and
  separate the 7×7 min-filter into row and column passes.

      11 +// taps. Scatter uses int32 atomicAdd on fixed point (scale 2^p
         +c.scale-ish,

         +o the result is

⏺ The phone and the Mac agree (54.8 dB; the small difference is the NPU prior
  and grain randomness), so quality is unchanged. Saving two lessons from today
  to memory:

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I ran the 8-frame night burst at full resolution on the phone. It found a
  crash and a memory leak, both fixed, and I made it faster without changing the
  image. But it is still slower than before: 126 s against the earlier 64 s. I
  also haven't measured capture-to-saved-file in the app yet; these numbers are
  the merge alone. All phone runs used the separate com.matthew.rawlens.lkrcheck
  package (build 2.1.76), not your app.

  Fixed:
  1. Crash: every merge failed on the phone. The GPU reported "device lost". One
     GPU pass (the adjoint scatter) didn't check whether a sample had moved
     outside the frame after that iteration's motion update, so it wrote outside
     its buffer. The Mac ignores that; the phone's GPU doesn't. One-line fix in
     scatter.comp.
  2. Memory leak: six buffers I added this session were never freed, about 40 MB
     per tile. Peak memory went from 3.67 GB to 1.23 GB, flat for the whole
     run. All solver buffers are now released on every exit path, including
     failures.
  3. Speed: four changes, each checked on the Mac (results 55–70 dB from before,
     the faster rendering of the adjoint passes was identical):
     - robust weights computed once per iteration instead of once per z-step;
     - the 7×7 spread filter split into a row pass and a column pass;
     - residual and weighted adjoint fused into one pass;
     - the robust and temporal neighbourhood sums split into cheaper separate
       passes.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬────────────────┬─────────┐
  │ 8-frame night burst on the phone │ Start of today │   Now   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 1024² crop                       │ 40.6 s         │ 18.9 s  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Full frame 4080×3060             │ failed / 141 s │ 126 s   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ GPU time (1024² crop)            │ 25.5 s         │ 12.7 s  │

  └──────────────────────────────────┴────────────────┴─────────┘

  Quality check: the phone's 1024² crop matches the Mac render at 54.8 dB. Edges
  are +1.8% and the colour means match to within 0.1%.

  Why it's still slower than 64 s: the earlier figure had none of the robust,
  temporal, local-motion or noise-equalisation passes, which are what fixed the
  ghosting, the water and the noise patches. What's left on the GPU:
  - the weighted adjoint, with its GPU atomics: about 4.8 s of the 12.7 s per
    1024² crop;
  - motion refinement: about 2.5 s.

  The NPU prior also queues: 504 calls at about 110 ms, shared by three workers.

  I spent part of this round chasing a false slowdown: a test setting I'd left
  on the phone was forcing a single worker. It's cleared now, the log prints the
  worker count, and I saved this to memory so it doesn't happen again.

  Nothing is committed.

  Next steps, in order of payoff:
  1. Measure capture to saved file in the app. This covers the parts outside the
     merge (frame copies, DNG writing), which this test doesn't include.
  2. Faster adjoint: replace the GPU atomics with a gather pass on the robust
     path. This is the biggest remaining GPU cost.
  3. Reuse solver buffers across tiles, instead of allocating about 30 per tile.

✻ Worked for 40m 25s · done Wednesday 4:40 PM

❯ I think our shadows have still too much chroma noise and luma  but especially 
  chroma too in daylight so for day maybe something a little bit cleaner        
  bringing it closer to MFSR but still keeping that "DSLR look"                 

⏺ Here's the plan. Daylight currently uses prior 0.15 and grain 0.8: a weak
  denoiser, with 80% of the removed luma texture added back. In shadows that
  leaves visible luma grain and almost untouched chroma noise. So I'll make the
  cleanup depend on the local signal level, so midtones and highlights keep the
  DSLR texture:
  - Chroma: a shadow-weighted, edge-aware chroma denoise.
  - Luma: grain that eases off where the signal is low.

  Starting with how the final look is applied:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I'll apply FFDNet with the solver's per-pixel noise map, raising denoising
  strength in shadows (where the signal-to-noise ratio is worst) on luma and
  chroma, and weighting it toward daylight so night is untouched.

⏺ The current robust gather adjoint has no local-motion term, so I'm writing a
  robust version that visits each HR pixel's reachable LR pixels and reads the
  residual and robust weight directly, with a fixed summation order and one
  extra LR pixel of search margin.

       1 +#version 450                                                   
       2 +#extension GL_GOOGLE_include_directive : require               
       3 +// SPDX-License-Identifier: GPL-3.0-or-later                   
       4 +// ROBUST data step as a GATHER (no atomics), fused with the HQ
         +S z-update:                                                    
       5 +//   z <- z - eta [U^T W r + mu (z - x)]                       
       6 +// r = residual (resid.comp: 0 = invalid / clipped), W = robust
         + weights wt.                                                   
       7 +// One invocation per HR pixel; for each frame the LR pixels wh
         +ose sub-sample                                                 
       8 +// taps can reach it lie within R = |A^-1|_inf / s of u* = A^-1
         + (q/s - t - d)                                                 
       9 +// (d = local block displacement at u*), widened by one LR pixe
         +l because d                                                    
      10 +// changes between neighbouring blocks. Each candidate's taps a
         +re recomputed                                                  
      11 +// EXACTLY as in resid.comp (affine + its own block's d), so th
         +e weights are                                                  
      12 +// the transpose of the forward operator. Replaces scatter.comp
         + /                                                             
      13 +// resid_adj_w.comp, whose int32 global atomics dominated GPU t
         +ime on Mali.                                                   
      14 +#define LKR_LOCAL_BINDING 4

      17 +layout(std430, binding = 0) buffer Z { float z[]; };

         +cationID.y);                                                   
      25 +  if (X0 >= pc.W || Y0 >= pc.H) return;                        
      26 +  float sf = float(pc.s);                                      
      27 +  float invn = 1.0 / (sf * sf);                                
      28 +  vec2 q0 = vec2(float(X0) + 0.5, float(Y0) + 0.5);            
      29 +  vec3 g = vec3(0.0);                                          
      30 +  for (int k = 0; k < pc.K; ++k) {                             
      31 +    float a0 = p[k * 6 + 0], a1 = p[k * 6 + 1], a2 = p[k * 6 +

         +umn-major inverse                                              
      35 +    vec2 us = Ai * (q0 / sf - vec2(a2, a5));                   
      36 +    us = Ai * (q0 / sf - vec2(a2, a5) - localD(k, us)); // firs
         +t guess -> with local d                                        
      37 +    float R = (max(abs(Ai[0][0]) + abs(Ai[1][0]), abs(Ai[0][1])

      38 +    int j0 = max(int(floor(us.x - R)), 0), j1 = min(int(floor(u
         +s.x + R)), pc.w - 1);                                          
      39 +    int i0 = max(int(floor(us.y - R)), 0), i1 = min(int(floor(u

⏺ Neither change made the phone faster.

  Gather adjoint: slower on the phone.
  - It produces the same image as the atomic version (61–70 dB, edges within
    0.03%).
  - On the Mali GPU it's 1.8× slower: 34.0 s against 18.6 s on the 1024² crop,
    repeated twice. Each HR pixel has to check about 25 candidate LR pixels per
    frame and recompute their taps, which costs more than Mali's atomics.
  - Narrowing that search window would at best tie the atomics and would make
    the adjoint inexact, so I didn't pursue it.
  - Atomics stay the default. The gather version stays in the code behind a
    switch (debug.lkr.rgather 1 on the phone, --rgather 1 on the Mac), in case a
    future GPU favours it.

  Buffer reuse: works, but no measurable speed gain.
  - Each tile worker now allocates its ~32 solver buffers once and reuses them,
    instead of on every one of the 63 tiles. Output is bit-identical.
  - The full frame took 127 s against 126 s before, within run-to-run noise.
    Memory peaks at 1.22 GB and stays flat.
  - Allocation was never a real cost; the time is in GPU compute (adjoint
    atomics, motion refinement) and NPU prior calls.

  So the full 8-frame merge on the phone is still about 127 s. Getting it much
  lower needs one of these:
  - Change the work itself: for example, refine motion on every second iteration
    instead of every iteration, or use bigger tiles with less overlap (about 7%
    fewer pixels). These change the result slightly, so they'd need a quality
    check like the earlier ones.
  - Measure capture to saved file in the app, to see how much time is outside
    the merge.

  Also, the daylight shadow change from earlier hasn't been run on the phone,
  and the full-resolution re-renders for 170823 and 111404 are still unfinished.
  Nothing is committed.

⏺ ESD4D is a large OpenGL ES merge with an optimisation history in its comments.
  Pulling out the performance and memory notes:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I read through ESD4D (PhotonCamera's merge, about 1,400 lines, commit b2eff82,
  7 Oct 2026). Most of its speed comes from being a lighter algorithm, not from
  tricks we can copy. Three of its techniques do apply.

  Why ESD4D is so much faster: it's a single-pass merge. Each frame is aligned
  once by a block pyramid, then blended into an accumulator with robust weights,
  so each frame goes through the GPU a handful of times. LK-SR solves an
  inverse problem: per tile it runs 16 iterations × 3 z-steps over all 8 frames,
  plus motion refinement and 8 prior calls. That gap can't be closed by
  implementation tricks, only narrowed.

  What applies to us:

  1. Bayer-quad packing (their biggest win, and the best fit for Mali). ESD4D
     stores every frame as a half-resolution texture where each texel holds one
     2×2 RGGB quad as a vec4. Every shader then runs 4× fewer invocations, and
     each fetch is one vector load instead of four scalar ones. My earlier Mali
     profiling (in memory) found this GPU is limited by load instructions, which
     is exactly what this cuts. In our solver all the per-LR-sample passes
     would benefit:
     - residual, weights, temporal test, min-filter;
     - the motion Jacobians;
     - the adjoint, where one invocation per quad would share the affine maths
       and local-motion lookups across 4 samples, though not the atomics
       themselves.

     Precision is unchanged (still float32). It's a sizeable refactor of about
     12 shaders plus the host indexing, checked the same way as today's changes.
  2. Normalise each frame once, up front. They convert every frame to normalised
     values once (NEON), and nothing later redoes black or white levels. We
     redo the conversion on the CPU for every tile, overlap included: the "prep"
     time measured 21–32 s summed over the workers. Converting the burst once
     at load time removes that, and frees each original buffer early.
  3. Scratch-buffer borrowing and early release. They lend one stage's scratch
     texture to another and free the burst as soon as it's on the GPU (their
     notes: −24 MB in one case, ~530 MB at 64 MP). For us that means dropping
     each raw frame once its float copy exists, and sharing scratch buffers
     between passes. That's memory only, not speed, but it brings the 1.2 GB
     peak down.

  What doesn't apply:
  - fp16 storage: it halves memory traffic, but our residuals and noise
    thresholds sit near fp16 precision in clean daylight, which would cost
    quality.
  - Their alignment pyramid: we already use sr-pipeline's block search, which is
    the same idea.
  - Running the network concurrently with alignment: we already overlap the NPU
    prior with GPU work across three workers.

  My recommendation is to do #2 first (small and safe), then #1 (the real GPU
  win, likely 1.5–2× on the GPU-bound part of the merge), and to check each on
  the phone against today's 127 s and 1.22 GB.

  I'll start on #2 and then #1 if you say go.

⏺ Neither the frame-conversion change nor the threading made the phone faster:
  the merge is GPU-bound, and CPU work isn't on its critical path.

  - #2 as proposed (normalise each frame once): not worth doing. Measured on the
    phone, the per-tile frame conversion (LoadTile) takes only 0.65 s of the
    28.9 s prep time, so I didn't build it.
  - The real CPU cost was the per-tile LK alignment (about 28 s summed over the
    workers). I parallelised it over frames, with up to 3 threads per worker.
    The output is bit-identical, and that CPU time fell from 28.9 s to 16.1 s.
  - Full-frame time is unchanged: 144 s this run, against 127–142 s in earlier
    runs, which is within the phone's run-to-run noise. Memory peak is 1.24 GB.

  The alignment threading is harmless, so I've kept it. It will start to matter
  once the GPU gets faster.

  Every measurement this session points the same way: wall time follows GPU
  time. So #1, Bayer-quad packing (ESD4D's main technique, aimed at exactly the
  load-instruction limit this Mali GPU hits) is the change that can actually cut
  the 127–144 s. It's the bigger refactor I described: about 12 shaders, same
  float32 precision, verified on the Mac and the phone like today's changes.

  Nothing is committed. Shall I start on #1?

✻ Worked for 8m 36s · done Wednesday 7:19 PM

❯ the                                                                           
  1. Bayer-quad packing (their biggest win, and the best fit for Mali). ESD4D   
  stores every frame as a half-resolution texture where each texel holds one    
  2×2 RGGB quad as a vec4. Every shader then runs 4× fewer invocations, and     
  each fetch is one vector load instead of four scalar ones. My earlier Mali    
  profiling (in memory) found this GPU is limited by load instructions, which   
  is exactly what this cuts. In our solver all the per-LR-sample passes would   
  benefit:                                                                      
     - residual, weights, temporal test, min-filter;                            
     - the motion Jacobians;                                                    
     - the adjoint, where one invocation per quad would share the affine maths  
  and local-motion lookups across 4 samples, though not the atomics themselves. 
       wo'nt lower our subpixel aligment SR part or something right ?           

⏺ No, it won't touch the sub-pixel alignment or the super-resolution. Quad
  packing only changes how the data is laid out in memory and how many samples
  one GPU thread handles. It doesn't change the maths.

  What stays exactly the same:
  - Every Bayer sample keeps its own exact position. One thread would handle the
    4 samples of a 2×2 quad, but each sample still gets its own sub-pixel
    position, its own warp (global affine plus its own block's local motion) and
    its own bilinear taps. Nothing is rounded to quad or half-resolution
    coordinates.
  - The forward model and its transpose are unchanged. Each sample is still
    compared against the high-resolution image, and its residual is still
    scattered back onto the same taps. That's where the super-resolution comes
    from: the 8 frames' sub-pixel offsets sample the scene at different
    positions.
  - The motion estimation is unchanged. It still uses every sample's own
    gradient and residual at its exact warped position, so alignment precision
    stays the same.

  What changes:
  - Four samples are loaded with one vector read instead of four scalar reads.
  - Shared work is done once per quad instead of four times: reading the frame's
    affine parameters, finding its local-motion block (all 4 samples are in the
    same 16-px block), and the bounds checks.
  - There are 4× fewer GPU threads, each doing 4 samples.

  One distinction from ESD4D: PhotonCamera's merge also aligns at quad level
  (half resolution) and blends whole quads. That suits a single-pass merge, but
  it would cost sub-pixel detail in ours, so I wouldn't copy it. Only the
  storage and threading layout comes over.

  How I'd verify it:
  - The output should match today's, apart from floating-point summation order.
    The atomic adjoint is fixed-point, so it stays exact; only the
    motion-Jacobian reductions might move in the last bits.
  - I'd require the same kind of result as today's changes (55–70 dB or better
    against the current output, edges and noise within 0.5%).
  - I'd check moving scenes (car, water) and static detail separately, and keep
    the paper-parity check passing.

  If any of those show a real difference, it's a bug, not a trade-off, and I'd
  fix it before measuring speed.

✻ Baked for 14s · done Wednesday 7:21 PM

❯ start on #1                                                                   
                                                                                
  1. Bayer-quad packing (their biggest win, and the best fit for Mali). ESD4D   
  stores every frame as a half-resolution texture where each texel holds one    
  2×2 RGGB quad as a vec4. Every shader then runs 4× fewer invocations, and     
  each fetch is one vector load instead of four scalar ones. My earlier Mali    
  profiling (in memory) found this GPU is limited by load instructions, which   
  is exactly what this cuts. In our solver all the per-LR-sample passes would   
  benefit:                                                                      
     - residual, weights, temporal test, min-filter;                            
     - the motion Jacobians;                                                    
     - the adjoint, where one invocation per quad would share the affine maths  
  and local-motion lookups across 4 samples, though not the atomics themselves. 
                                                                                
     Precision is unchanged (still float32). It's a sizeable refactor of about  
  12 shaders plus the host indexing, checked the same way as today's changes.   

⏺ Starting on it. To keep the risk low and the host code untouched, I'll do it
  in two layers:
  - One GPU thread per 2×2 Bayer quad. It handles the quad's 4 samples, sharing
    the affine parameters and the local-motion lookup. All 4 samples always fall
    in the same 16-px motion block, so this is exact.
  - vec2 loads and stores over the existing buffer layout. Each quad's row pair
    is two adjacent floats, so a quad costs 2 vector loads instead of 4 scalar
    ones. No host indexing changes.

  Each sample still keeps its own sub-pixel position and taps. I'll start with
  the three hottest per-sample passes (the residual, the fused weighted adjoint,
  and the scatter), measure them on the phone, and then do the rest.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Saving this lesson to memory so I don't repeat it:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Bayer-quad packing didn't make LK-SR any faster on the phone, so it's built
  but off by default.

  What I built: quad versions of the three hottest per-sample passes: the
  residual, the fused weighted adjoint and the scatter.
  - One GPU thread handles a whole 2×2 Bayer quad, reads its row pairs as vec2
    loads, and does the affine and local-motion lookup once per quad.
  - Each sample keeps its own exact sub-pixel position and taps, as promised.
  - The output matches the per-sample version to rounding level: edges +0.00 to
    +0.04%, flat noise within +0.25%, no pixel differs by more than 0.01 (except
    0.002% in the night crop).

  Phone result on the 1024² crop, alternated twice: 19.5 / 19.9 s with quads
  against 20.9 / 20.2 s per-sample, which is run-to-run noise. Per pass, the
  residual and the fused adjoint got slightly slower and only the scatter got
  slightly faster.

  Why: in these passes the cost is the bilinear reads of the high-resolution
  image at warped positions, plus the GPU atomics. Both happen once per sample
  whatever the layout. My earlier finding that this GPU is limited by load
  instructions came from the video demosaic shaders, which read in neat
  neighbourhoods, and it doesn't carry over here. I've saved that to memory.

  The quad passes and the gather adjoint both stay in the code as switches
  (debug.lkr.quad 1, debug.lkr.rgather 1), each documented with its measurement.
  Nothing is committed.

  The full frame is still about 127–144 s on the phone. What did help today was
  doing fewer passes: weights once per iteration, the fused residual-adjoint,
  and the separable filters. Implementation tricks are largely used up, so a
  real gain now means less work per tile. Each of these changes the result
  slightly and would need the same quality checks as before:
  1. Refine motion every second iteration instead of every one. Motion
     refinement is about 20% of GPU time and settles after a few iterations.
  2. Bigger tiles with less relative overlap: about 7% fewer pixels to solve.
  3. Compute the temporal weights every second iteration: they only depend on
     motion, which barely changes late in the solve.

  I'd try #1 first and check it against the moving and static scenes. Should I
  go ahead?

⏺ Refining motion every second iteration works with no visible quality cost, but
  it saves only about 2–8% of time on the phone, much less than I estimated.
  I've kept it as the new default.

  What changed: motion is refined on every iteration for the first 4 (while it's
  still settling), then on every second one. That's 10 refinements per tile
  instead of 16. Paper parity still refines every iteration, and its check
  passes.

  Quality, against every-iteration refinement:

  ┌─────────────────────┬────────┬────────────┬─────────┬────────────┐
  │        Crop         │ Edges  │ Flat noise │ Texture │ Difference │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────┼────────────┤

  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────┼────────────┤

  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────┼────────────┤
  │ Roof (static, 2×)   │ +0.02% │ −1.0%      │ 0.0%    │ 55.1 dB    │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────┼────────────┤
  │ Night building      │ +0.07% │ −1.7%      │ 0.0%    │ 66.2 dB    │
  └─────────────────────┴────────┴────────────┴─────────┴────────────┘

  On the moving car the two versions look identical. Only 0.03% of pixels differ
  by more than 0.005, all on the car's edges and headlights. Refining only
  every third iteration drifts further (night noise −2.8%), so I didn't use it.

  Speed on the phone:
  - 1024² crop, alternated twice: 23.3 → 21.4 s and 20.3 → 19.9 s.
  - Full frame: 129 s, inside the 127–144 s range of earlier runs. Memory peak
    is 1.22 GB.

  The gain is small because cutting 6 of 16 refinements removes only about a
  third of the motion work. Motion work itself is about 20% of GPU time, so the
  saving is about 7% of GPU time, which the phone's run-to-run noise mostly
  hides.

  Where this leaves speed: of the three ideas I listed, this was expected to be
  the biggest. The other two (bigger tiles, temporal weights every second
  iteration) are each worth about 5–7%, so even together they would bring the
  full frame to roughly 110–115 s at best. Getting under a minute would need the
  method itself to do less, for example fewer total iterations or prior calls.
  That changes the look and would need your judgement on the images, not just my
  metrics.

  Nothing is committed.

✻ Brewed for 9m 50s · done Wednesday 7:44 PM

❯ Where this leaves speed: of the three ideas I listed, this was expected to be 
  the biggest. The other two (bigger tiles, temporal weights every second       
  iteration) are each worth about 5–7%, so even together they would bring the   
  full frame to roughly 110–115 s at best. Getting under a minute would need    
  the method itself to do less, for example fewer total iterations or prior     
  calls. That changes the look and would need your judgement on the images, not 
  just my metrics. is it worth it?                                              

⏺ Partly. I wouldn't do the two small tweaks, but one quick experiment on
  iteration count is worth running before deciding.

  Skip the two 5–7% tweaks. Each one adds another switch and a small quality
  risk for a gain the phone's run-to-run noise mostly hides. Together they'd
  still leave about 110–115 s.

  Test fewer iterations. Almost all the remaining cost scales with the iteration
  count: GPU passes, motion and prior calls all repeat per iteration. Going
  from 16 iterations to 12 would cut about a quarter of the time, and to 10
  about 35–40%. That puts the full frame around 80–95 s, which is a meaningful
  change.

  The question is whether the image changes:
  - The noise schedule runs geometrically from 10× to 1× the noise level. The
    last few iterations work at small noise levels and mostly polish.
  - The first iterations do the heavy lifting: alignment and the large-scale
    image.
  - So 12 iterations with 6 prior calls may look nearly identical to 16 with 8.
    10 might start to show as slightly less fine detail or different grain.

  I can't tell from metrics alone where "nearly identical" ends, which is where
  your eye comes in.

  Running it is cheap: on the Mac, render 16 / 12 / 10 iterations for a few
  bursts (car, water, the shaded wall, the night burst) and put them side by
  side. It's about half an hour, and there's no phone work until you pick one.

  It also depends on how LK-SR is used. The merge runs in the background after
  capture, like MFSR and HDR+, so the camera stays usable meanwhile. If you
  shoot occasional LK-SR photos, 2 minutes in the background may be acceptable
  and not worth any look change. If you shoot several in a row, they queue, and
  a faster solve matters more.

  One more lever that doesn't touch the look at all: the zoom mode we designed 
  earlier (1.5× / 2× crop, output always at sensor resolution). A 2× crop solves
  a quarter of the area at 2× scale, so it would finish in well under a minute
  on the phone.

  Shall I run the 16 / 12 / 10 comparison?

  the phone's screen again without asking.

  LK-SR quick tile: LK-SR now has its own tile next to HDR+ and MFSR, replacing
  the old MFSR → LK-SR cycle.
  - Tapping a tile turns that engine on, and tapping it again turns it off.
  - Tapping the other engine's tile switches straight over to it.
  - Both are mutually exclusive with HDR+ and the HDR bracket, as before.
  - Unit tests for the two tiles pass.

  New Settings → Merge tab (between Burst and Denoise):

    ZSL ring and sidecars.
  - LK-SR: the paper-parity switch, plus "Show LK-SR Lab (expert tuning)", which
    is off by default and hides the expert controls.

  LK-SR Lab has 28 controls, sliders and switches:
  - iterations, data steps, how often the denoiser runs;
  - the day and night look (denoise and grain);
  - motion rejection (threshold, edge tolerance, spread);
  - the reference-frame check (used for moving water), with its own edge
    tolerance;
  - noise evening, local motion and its stiffness, how often motion is refined,
    per-tile alignment;
  - colour preservation, daylight shadow cleanup (luma and colour);
  - Bayer pattern filter, clip level, start demosaic, step size;
  - tile overlap, parallel tiles, schedule start.

  Each control has a one-line explanation. There's also a reset button.

  How the defaults work:
  - The sliders show the defaults straight from the native code, so they can't
    drift from what the merge actually uses.
  - Only values you change are saved. Setting one back to its default forgets
    the override, so future code updates apply again.
  - Changes apply to the next LK-SR merge, live and offline.
  - Paper parity still overrides everything in the Lab.

  What I verified on the phone (.lkrcheck package, version 2.1.97): an LK-SR
  merge runs through the changed native interface, and both native functions are
  exported correctly.

  What I couldn't verify: the new tile and the Merge tab on screen. Could you
  check them when you have a moment? Or tell me when the phone is free and I'll
  do it.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The paper-parity default is now locked in, and the build plus unit tests pass.
  On Vulkan: switching to 1.2 or 1.3 wouldn't make LK-SR faster or use less
  memory on its own. The phone's driver already supports Vulkan 1.3.247, so
  moving up is easy, but the API version doesn't change what the GPU does. Only
  specific new features could help, and I checked which ones this driver offers.

  Paper parity: the code already defaulted to the tuned version everywhere. The
  only risk was an old saved "on" value from earlier testing or a backup. The
  switch now uses a new preference key, so any stale value is ignored once and
  every install starts on the tuned path. A unit test pins it: fresh settings,
  empty preferences and a stale old "on" value all give the tuned version, and
  the switch still works when you use it.

  Vulkan features on this phone:

  Feature: Float atomics (VK_EXT_shader_atomic_float)
  Available: no
  Would it help LK-SR?: Could have replaced our fixed-point atomics, but the
    driver doesn't offer it
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: fp16 maths and 16-bit storage
  Available: yes
  Would it help LK-SR?: Halves memory and bandwidth, but costs quality: our
    residuals and noise thresholds sit near fp16 precision in clean daylight
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: Timeline semaphores, synchronization2
  Available: yes
  Would it help LK-SR?: Only lighter CPU–GPU syncing. Wall time follows GPU
  time,
    so no measurable gain
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: Subgroup size control
  Available: yes
  Would it help LK-SR?: Our one subgroup reduction is already fast; nothing big
    left there
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: Buffer device address
  Available: yes
  Would it help LK-SR?: A convenience for pointers in shaders, not a speed
    feature
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: Cooperative matrix (tensor-style maths)
  Available: no
  Would it help LK-SR?: Not applicable anyway

  Why none of it moves the needle: profiling today showed the time goes to
  bilinear reads of the high-resolution image at warped positions, and to
  integer atomics. Those are hardware limits of this GPU, and the API version
  doesn't change them.

  fp16 storage for memory only: it could store the eight input frames at half
  size (roughly 200 MB less) without touching the solver's arithmetic. The cost
  is a quarter-step rounding of each raw value, so I'd only do it if memory
  becomes a problem. Today it peaks at about 1.2 GB and stays flat.

  Nothing is committed, and the on-screen check of the new tile and Merge tab is
  still waiting for you.

⏺ I went through every LK-SR shader with malioc. The one optimisation it pointed
  to made the phone slower, so I've turned it off. malioc won't take LK-SR much
  further on this GPU.

  What malioc shows: every shader is limited by memory loads, with almost no
  arithmetic cost. None spill to the stack. Only the two motion shaders (jac,
  jacblk) have a register problem: 64 registers, which halves how many threads
  the GPU keeps in flight.

  What I tried: malioc counts load instructions, so I packed each pixel's 4
  bilinear taps into one vector. The heavy passes then read one value instead of
  four, with bit-identical output.
  - malioc confirmed it: load cycles fell 30% in the residual and 20% in the
    fused adjoint.
  - On the phone (1024² crop) it was slower: 20.9 / 23.3 s against 20.2 / 20.3
    s.
  - Per pass, the adjoint went from 3.0 to 4.5 s and the residual from 1.2 to
    2.5 s.

  Why: the packed copy repeats neighbouring pixels, so it's 4× the bytes, and
  the GPU cache holds far less of it. The original four loads mostly hit the
  same one or two cache lines, which is cheap. So these passes are limited by
  bytes moved and cache misses, which malioc doesn't model. That's the same
  reason the quad packing and the gather adjoint didn't help earlier.

  The packing stays in the code as an off-by-default switch (debug.lkr.pack),
  documented with these numbers, and I've saved the lesson to memory. Nothing is
  committed.

  What's left from malioc: only the motion shaders' 64 registers. Fixing them is
  worth about 1.5 s of GPU time per 1024² crop at best, because motion now runs
  every second iteration. It would mean restructuring how the motion sums are
  accumulated. Given today's results, I'd only try it if you want every last
  bit.

  The two levers that would really move phone time are fewer solver iterations
  (which changes the look slightly, so you'd judge it on images) and the zoom
  mode (less area to solve).

  Separately, the new LK-SR tile and the Merge settings tab still need a look on
  screen. Tell me when the phone is free, or check them yourself.

✻ Brewed for 9m 16s · done Wednesday 8:23 PM

❯ On zoom mode: yes, I'll add it. Here's the design, so you can correct         
  anything before I build it:                                                   
                                                                                
  Zoom chip in the viewfinder, visible when LK-SR is the active method: tap to  
  cycle 1× → 1.5× → 2×.                                                         
  Preview zooms to match (a centre crop of the live view), so you frame what    
  you'll get.                                                                   
  Merge:                                                                        
                                                                                
  At 1×: the full frame, solved at ×1, as now.                                  
  At 2×: the centre half of the sensor (2040×1530), solved at ×2, giving        
  exactly 4080×3060.                                                            
  At 1.5×: the centre 2720×2040 crop solved at ×2 (5440×4080), then downsampled 
  to 4080×3060. That keeps the real detail gain.                                
  Output is always the sensor resolution, 12.5 MP on your phone, as             
  DNG/JPEG/JXL through the same savers, with lens shading mapped to the crop's  
  position on the sensor.                                                       
  Speed: a 2× zoom is 1/4 of the sensor area per frame, so roughly a quarter of 
  the ×2 work of a full frame.                                                  
                                                                                
  That covers live capture in every mode (it's the same MFSR shutter path). The 
  Merges screen gets the same 1× / 1.5× / 2× choice for staged bursts.

      28 +    }                                                          
      29 +  }

          +ShadingModel,

⏺ Zoom mode works end to end on the phone. But it's slower than my design
  implied: 2× takes about as long as a full frame at 1×, not less.

  Phone result, 8-frame night burst:

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬───────────┐
  │     Zoom      │    Crop solved at ×2     │     Output      │  Merge    │
  │               │                          │                 │   time    │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────┤
  │               │ centre 2040×1530 (20     │ 4080×3060       │           │
  │ 2×            │ tiles)                   │ (checked by the │ 140 s     │
  │               │                          │  test)          │           │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────┤
  │               │ centre 2720×2040 →       │                 │           │
  │ 1.5×          │ 5440×4080,               │ 4080×3060       │ 205 s     │
  │               │ area-downsampled         │                 │           │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 1× (for       │ full frame at ×1         │ 4080×3060       │ about     │
  │ comparison)   │                          │                 │ 127–144 s │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────┴───────────┘

  Speed: my design note said 2× would be "roughly a quarter of the ×2 work of a
  full frame". That's true, but a ×2 solve costs about 4× a ×1 solve per sensor
  pixel, so 2× zoom lands at about the same time as a normal full-frame merge,
  and 1.5× is about 1.5× longer. Nothing is broken; the estimate was misleading.

  What I built:
  - Viewfinder chip "SR 1×" at the end of the lens row. It shows only while
    LK-SR is the active merge in photo mode; tapping cycles 1× → 1.5× → 2×, and
    it's highlighted when not 1×.
  - Preview zoom: the viewfinder scales about its centre to match, so you frame
    exactly the crop that gets solved. Capture is unchanged, so every frame is
    still full sensor.
  - Merge:
    - 2×: centre crop solved at ×2.
    - 1.5×: centre crop solved at ×2, then an exact area-average downsample to
      sensor size in native code.
    - Lens shading is mapped to each pixel's real position on the sensor.
    - The same DNG/JPEG/JXL savers are used. It applies to live capture in every
      mode.
  - Merges screen: a "LK-SR ZOOM" row (1× / 1.5× / 2×) for staged bursts. It has
    its own remembered choice, separate from the live chip, and shows only when
    LK-SR is the selected method.
  - Tests: unit tests for the crop geometry (centred, Bayer-aligned, sensor-size
    output, saved choice), plus the phone test cases zoom2 and zoom15.

  One known limit: at 1.5× and 2×, tap-to-focus positions still map to the
  unzoomed preview. A tap focuses where that point would be at 1×. I can map the
  coordinates if you want it.

  Nothing is committed. Still waiting for you to look at on screen: the LK-SR
  tile, the Merge settings tab and LK-SR Lab, and now the zoom chip. I'll keep
  off the phone's screen unless you tell me it's free.

⏺ The smear in the trees and on the bunker came from merging frames where the 
  branches had moved in the wind. I've fixed most of it. The new DNG is at
  ~/Downloads/LKSR_all/IMG_20261104_111404_842_LKSR.dng, to open in RawTherapee
  next to the MFSR one. Nothing is committed.

  How I found it:
  - LK-SR on four copies of the reference frame, where nothing can move, has no 
    streaks. So frame motion causes them, not the denoiser or grain.
  - On the pine, the other frames were only half-rejected (mean weight 0.57,
    only 8% strongly rejected). About 5 slightly displaced frames got blended,
    which is motion blur.
  - The softly shaped rejection curve was the problem. MFSR rejects harder,
    which is why it looked clean.

  What changed:
  1. Steeper motion rejection in daylight. Frames that agree keep full weight,
     and wind-moved ones drop out quickly instead of blending at half strength.
     - On the pine, the distance to the motion-free result fell 40% (0.0018 →
       0.0011).
     - Static detail is unchanged (edges −0.1 to −0.5% on window and roof).
       Daylight flat noise rises slightly (+2 to 6%).
     - It blends back to the old curve at night. A steep curve at night smoothed
       the night building 20%, so night is exactly as before.
  2. Gentler noise evening-out in daylight: 0.25 strength in daylight, full
     strength at night. The extra smoothing in rejected areas had been
     flattening stone texture where the tree's shadow moved across the bunker.
     The moving-car scene still shows no noise patches.
  3. The LK-SR Lab threshold slider now fixes the threshold at all light levels
     when you set it. Its default shows the daylight value of 2. Paper parity
     still passes, and the Android build compiles.

  What I tried and kept off:
  - Sharpest frame as reference: it picked frame 1 and made the pine worse,
    because wind blur is local to the trees.
  - A strict reference check: it removed the streaks but made static areas
    plastic (edges −5 to −11%).

  Still open:
  - Faint streaks remain at the top right of the pine. Fully hard rejection
    removes them, but costs super-resolution everywhere. Fixing the rest
    properly needs rejection that knows a region is moving rather than one with
    fine static texture.
  - The daylight shadow cleanup (from your earlier "cleaner shadows" request)
    also softens shaded foliage a little. Its colour part is harmless, but you
    might prefer a weaker luma part. Tell me if the shaded trees still look too
    smooth to you.

      72 +// In-place separable box blur of a square grey patch (radius r
         +, clamped edges).                                              
      73 +static void BoxBlurGrey(std::vector<float>* a, int n, int r) { 
      74 +  std::vector<float> t(size_t(n) * n);                         
      75 +  for (int pass = 0; pass < 2; ++pass) {                       
      76 +    const std::vector<float>& s = pass == 0 ? *a : t;          
      77 +    std::vector<float>& d = pass == 0 ? t : *a;                
      78 +    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)                                
      79 +      for (int j = 0; j < n; ++j) {                            
      80 +        double acc = 0;                                        
      81 +        for (int k = -r; k <= r; ++k) {                        
      82 +          const int jj = std::min(std::max(j + k, 0), n - 1);  
      83 +          acc += pass == 0 ? s[size_t(i) * n + jj] : s[size_t(j
         +j) * n + i];                                                   
      84 +        }                                                      
      85 +        d[pass == 0 ? size_t(i) * n + j : size_t(j) * n + i] = 
         +float(acc / (2 * r + 1));                                      
      86 +      }                                                        
      87 +  }                                                            
      88 +}                                                              
      89 +

  - Daylight (5 bursts): LK-SR keeps more edge contrast and texture than MFSR
    everywhere. It keeps more fine grain (noise 0.47–0.68 against MFSR's
    0.24–0.39), which is the intended "DSLR look", while MFSR is smoother. The

  HDR+ and the reference, looking for problems I don't know about yet.

  Learned so far on 5a9e:
  1. The painterly smear in your grass and shadows wasn't a merging problem.
     LK-SR on four copies of the reference frame, with no motion possible,
     smeared just the same.
  2. The cause was the luma part of my daylight shadow cleanup. It raises the
     denoiser's strength in "shadows" and turns fine grass into brush strokes.
     Fixed: it's now off by default (still adjustable in LK-SR Lab). The colour
     part stays, and colour noise remains below Google's.
  3. "Shadow" is judged by absolute raw level, so in an HDR scene exposed for
     the sky the whole foreground counts. That's now harmless for luma, but the
     colour cleanup uses the same definition and should look at local
     signal-to-noise instead. I'll check that in the next pass.
  4. The desktop MFSR CLI writes mirrored DNGs for BGGR and RGGB sensors. The
     app isn't affected.
  5. The local-motion field drifts up to 8 px in flat sky and sea. It's harmless
     on flat areas, but I'll check whether it hurts near edges in the other
     bursts.

  None of today's changes are committed yet: the shadow default, the debug maps,
  and the earlier sub-tile, gate and highlight work. I'll commit them to
  refactor/2.0 once this iteration round is done.

⏺ Yes, a plan is worth it, but a selective one. The paper is "High Dynamic Range
  and Super-Resolution from Raw Image Bursts" by Lecouat, Eboli, Ponce and
  Mairal (SIGGRAPH 2022), the direct successor of LK-Reloaded. Several of its
  ideas transfer to our version without training anything; the rest depends on
  learned networks we don't have.

  What it changes relative to LK-Reloaded:
  - Exposure-bracketed bursts: frames with different exposure times are merged
    into one HDR, super-resolved image. The forward model gains each frame's
    exposure time and a saturation mask.
  - Alignment: 200×200-px tiles, each with its own 6-parameter affine. A 4-level
    pyramid Lucas-Kanade runs on features from a tiny learned U-Net. That alone
    halves the alignment error (2.04 against 3.82 HR px).
  - Learned confidence network: it compares each frame with the warped reference
    and down-weights misaligned frames. This replaces hand-tuned rejection.
  - Merge weights: proportional to exposure time (longer means less noise), with
    saturated samples weighted zero.
  - Starting image: a classic aligned, weighted-average merge, instead of one
    demosaiced frame.
  - Only 3 solver stages instead of our 16, because the step sizes and the prior
    are trained.
  - Results: +1 dB over LK-Reloaded on ×4 super-resolution and about 3× lower
    alignment error. Their stated limits: small scene motion only, and non-rigid
    motion such as a waving flag still blurs.

  Worth taking, without any training:
  1. Weighted-average merge as the start image, then fewer iterations. We
     currently start from one demosaiced frame, so the solver spends many of its
     16 iterations just pulling in the other frames. Starting from an aligned
     merge could let us drop to 8–10 iterations with the same look: a direct
     phone speed-up, measurable on our bursts and the HDR+ set.
  2. Alignment on denoised or gradient features instead of raw grey. It's a
     cheap stand-in for their learned features. Today's lessons point the same
     way: misalignment and parallax were real causes of smear, and at night only
     blurred patches aligned reliably.
  3. Their saturation handling and exposure-time weights. We already mask
     clipping and compensate gain; their formulation is a small, clean extension
     of that.

  Worth it only as a feature decision:
  4. HDR from exposure brackets. This would be a new capture mode, LK-SR HDR,
  with real highlight recovery on top of super-resolution. The solver change is
  modest, but capture would need bracketed bursts in the LK-SR path (the app's
  HDR bracket tile already captures brackets). That's a product decision for
  you: bigger, but the most visible gain.

  Not now:
  5. Training their confidence network and prior. It would need a differentiable
  training pipeline (our python/lkr torch solver is a starting point),
  synthetic training data, and weeks of work.

  My recommendation: a plan for items 1–3 first. They're measurable against
  Google HDR+ on the dataset we just set up, and item 1 attacks the phone speed
  directly. Item 4 is up to you.

  Separately, the debug-map batch for all 8 HDR+ bursts has finished, and I
  haven't reviewed those maps yet; that's still open from your previous request.
  Today's code changes (shadow cleanup default, debug maps, sub-tile alignment,
  night gate, highlight cleanup) aren't committed yet either.

  Should I write up the plan for items 1–3, or review the maps first?
